alt="Sagittal T2 MRI of the lumbar spine with a scroll-driven color overlay indicating increasing prevalence of age-related findings">

Model predictions from Brinjikji et al. 2015, AJNR. Values between decades are linearly interpolated by us. Hatched bars at ages 20 to 30: low data. Facet degeneration and spondylolisthesis come from the same model and are not charted here; the journey chapter below carries the facet note.

AGE 20

The first frame

Disk degeneration 37% · bulge 30% · protrusion 29% · annular fissure 19%. Even at 20, pain-free people already carry these words on their scans.

AGE 30

The vocabulary grows

Disk degeneration 52% · bulge 40% · protrusion 31% · annular fissure 20%.

AGE 40

“Abnormal” becomes ordinary

Disk degeneration 68% · bulge 50% · protrusion 33% · annular fissure 22%. Most of the bars climb together now. That is aging shifting the background, not injury.

AGE 50

The scan accumulates words

Disk degeneration 80% · bulge 60% · protrusion 36% · annular fissure 23%. A longer report still does not name the source of your pain.

AGE 60

Most discs look different

Disk degeneration 88% · bulge 69% · protrusion 38% · annular fissure 25%. The overlay tracks how common a finding is, not how much it hurts.

AGE 70

The base rate dominates

Disk degeneration 93% · bulge 77% · protrusion 40% · annular fissure 27%. A finding can be real and still need the history and exam to mean anything.

AGE 80

Near saturation

Disk degeneration 96% · bulge 84% · protrusion 43% · annular fissure 29%. The scan is detailed. What caused the pain still belongs to the history and the examination.

Watch the “abnormalities” arrive.

Each card is one line a radiologist might write. Flip it, and the scary vocabulary becomes a base rate: how often the same feature shows up in people with no back pain.

An “abnormal” MRI is the normal result.

NORMAL
FOR AGE
About the mock reportIt sums the four group prevalences to estimate an average count. It does not assume the findings are independent and never calculates a joint probability.
The curve

The gray hair of the spine

Four model-predicted prevalence curves. Tap a label to isolate a finding. The source only reports decade values, so we joined them with straight lines.

Percent of asymptomatic individuals · Brinjikji et al., AJNR 2015, Table 2.

The words

“Degeneration” and “bulge” are descriptions.

Radiology needs words for shape, signal, and wear. Those words can be useful without being a verdict on pain. Brinjikji and colleagues concluded1: “Many imaging-based degenerative features are likely part of normal aging and unassociated with pain…”

Jensen’s 1994 echo2: among 98 people without back pain, 52% had a disk bulge at at least one level and 27% had a protrusion.

So don't toss the scan. Use it for a different question: does this finding fit the history, examination, and neurologic pattern?

Language ≠ mechanismA structural finding can matter, be incidental, or sit inside a bigger pain process. Only context decides which.
Words vs. meaning

Decode the report

Three common phrases, translated in plain words.

Degenerative disc disease
Wear visible on scan
96% of pain-free 80-year-olds: disk degeneration
Disc bulge
The disc extends broadly beyond its usual margin
50% of pain-free 40-year-olds
Annular fissure
A line or separation in the disc’s outer ring
29% of pain-free 80-year-olds
Counterweight

Painful spines are not identical.

Most findings are common, but some still track with pain. A companion meta-analysis (Brinjikji et al., AJNR 2015) pooled 14 case-control studies of 3,097 adults aged 50 or younger (1,193 without back pain, 1,904 with) and found disc bulge, disc degeneration, extrusion and protrusion, Modic type 1 change, and spondylolysis enriched in painful spines. Annular fissure (OR 1.79, P=.06) and spondylolisthesis (OR 1.59, P=.20) missed statistical significance. Read those odds ratios as describing that pooled group of adults 50 or younger. They are not any one reader's risk, and they say nothing about spines over 50.

The takeawayBase rates keep you from overcalling. Association data keep you from dismissing everything. Neither replaces the patient in front of you.
The journey

One early scan, seven stops

The findings are only the second stop. Here is the whole trip an early MRI can start, in order, with the strongest number this essay can source at each stop, the people that number describes, and an outcome at the end: surgery, not just activity.

  1. 1

    Scan

    Back pain starts, and an MRI is ordered in the first days or weeks, well before the six-week mark the ACR Appropriateness Criteria set for uncomplicated pain.

    Up to 20% of uncomplicated low back pain patients still received an early MRI in the studies from 2010 to 2014.

    POPULATION: uncomplicated low back pain, observational studies summarized in the ACR criteria review.

  2. 2

    Findings

    The report describes the spine in fine detail, and most of what it names is age, not injury. At 50, disk degeneration appears in 80 of 100 pain-free people and a disk bulge in 60 of 100. Those are the background rates the report is written against.

    80% disk degeneration at age 50, among people with no back pain at all.

    POPULATION: 3,110 pain-free people across 33 studies (Brinjikji et al., AJNR 2015).

    One finding is not on the chart above: facet degeneration. In the same model it climbs from rarely reported under 40 to 83% of pain-free 80-year-olds. Its youngest values (4% at 20, 9% at 30) are model extrapolations from zero direct observations, so read them as the model's guess, not a count. It is plotted nowhere in this essay because the essay charts the four findings with direct observations behind them.

  3. 3

    Concern

    The words land as damage. “Degeneration” sounds like crumbling. “Bulge” sounds like something about to rupture. But the vocabulary was there before the pain: in Jensen's 1994 study, 52% of people with no back pain had a bulge at at least one level and 27% had a protrusion.

    POPULATION: 98 volunteers without back pain (Jensen et al., NEJM 1994); their scans were read independently by two neuroradiologists who did not know anyone's clinical status.

  4. 4

    Consult

    The finding, more than the pain, becomes the reason for the next appointments: a surgical consult, a follow-up scan, an injection to consider. This essay has no sourced count for this stop. It is the hinge of the journey, not a measured rate, and it is labeled that way rather than given an invented number.

  5. 5

    Imaging cascade

    Care accumulates around the early scan. In the observational data, the early-MRI group's average total costs ran about 3.3 times the comparison group's.

    $22,151 vs $6,640 average total costs, early MRI vs no early MRI ($15,511 more per person).

    POPULATION: uncomplicated low back pain, observational cohorts from 2010 to 2014, summarized in the ACR criteria review. Association, not proof of cause.

  6. 6

    Confirmed serious disease

    This is the stop the scan exists for. Cancer, infection, fracture, and cauda equina syndrome are the diagnoses imaging must not miss, and confirming one changes everything. They are uncommon in uncomplicated presentations, which is why the red-flag chapter below, not routine scanning, does the sorting.

  7. 7

    Surgery

    The journey ends in an outcome. In the same observational data, the early-MRI group reached surgery far more often: about 8 times the rate of the group without an early scan, or roughly 17 more operations per 100 people.

    19.9% vs 2.5% had surgery, early MRI vs no early MRI.

    POPULATION: same observational cohorts as stop 5. Association, not proof of cause.

OBSERVATIONAL ASSOCIATION, NOT CAUSATION. People who received an early MRI differed from people who did not. Stops 5 and 7 describe what traveled together, not proof that the scan caused the later care.

Red flags

Do not scan every back. Do not miss the dangerous one.

ACR guidance3: for uncomplicated acute low back pain, no imaging for the first six weeks. The timeline changes when any of these show up:

  • Suspicion of cancer
  • Suspicion of infection
  • Cauda equina syndrome or other severe neurologic compromise
  • Fracture

Severe or progressive neurologic deficits, and symptoms that persist despite appropriate management, also change the timeline and the imaging strategy.

Not a checklist diagnosisRed flags guide clinical evaluation. Individual features vary in accuracy and must be interpreted together.
Educational reference

Four reasons to change course

No quiz, score, or symptom checker, just plain-language reasons clinicians may image urgently.

Red flags are uncommon. That’s why they matter.

Educational only; talk to a clinician.

Reporting

What if the report carried its own context?

The LIRE trial4 (NCT02015455) tested a simple fix: print age-specific prevalence benchmarks for common findings right inside lumbar imaging reports. Instead of leaving “bulge” alone on the page, the report shows how often it appears in people without pain.

LIRENCT02015455

Some systems now print these age-expected numbers inside radiology reports, putting epidemiology beside anatomy at the moment decisions begin.

Your MRI isn’t lying about your spine. It’s just not telling you about your pain.

Context is an interventionNumbers can change how a report is read without changing a single pixel of the scan.

  2. Jensen MC, et al. Magnetic Resonance Imaging of the Lumbar Spine in People Without Back Pain. NEJM. 1994;331:69-73. Among 98 people without back pain, 52% had a disk bulge at one or more levels. ↩

Sources

What these numbers can and cannot say

  • These are cross-sectional group prevalences, not individual predictions.
  • “Asymptomatic” was defined heterogeneously across 33 studies: 32 MRI studies and 1 CT study.
  • Finding severity was not stratified in the age-prevalence table.
  • Slider values between decades are linear interpolations added for this visualization.
  • Overuse figures are observational associations, not proof that early MRI caused later care.
  • Chart data: the four age-prevalence series are in data/mri-back-pain/.

  2. Jensen MC, et al. Magnetic Resonance Imaging of the Lumbar Spine in People Without Back Pain. NEJM. 1994;331:69 to 73.
  3. Brinjikji W, et al. MRI Findings of Disc Degeneration Are More Prevalent in Adults with Low Back Pain than in Asymptomatic Controls. AJNR. 2015. Full text reviewed for the counterweight panel: a meta-analysis of 14 case-control studies, 3,097 adults aged 50 or younger. An earlier version of this page mislabeled this article as abstract-only; it is a full journal article.
  4. ACR Appropriateness Criteria: Low Back Pain. Imaging timing and red-flag indications.
  5. Lumbar Imaging Reporting with Epidemiology (LIRE), NCT02015455.

Educational, not medical advice; talk to a clinician. Seek urgent care for new bladder or bowel dysfunction, saddle-area numbness, rapidly progressive weakness, or other concerning symptoms.

Essay 02 · Animated epidemiology

The typical spinal cord injury patient is 15 years older than in 1973.

By a PM&R resident physician

Why this essay: Spinal cord injury is the heart of physiatry, and its demographics shifted while nobody was looking.

Surviving the injury was only the first change. The patients got older, the causes tilted toward falls, and the rehab system built around the young trauma patient has not caught up.
